Spherical bipolar fuzzy decision model for green infrastructure selection
Authors: Kannusamy Aarthi, Samayan Narayanamoorthy, Navaneethakrishnan Suganthi Keerthana Devi, Krishnan Suvitha, Naif Almakayeel, Hasan Dincer, Serhat Yuksel, Daekook Kang
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
Increasing urbanization, brought on by population expansion and industrialization, is posing a number of environmental concerns for urban and suburban areas. Urban communities are adapting to the intricacies brought about by a number of factors, including pollution, temperature variations, and climate change. However, achieving adaptation sustainably remains a significant challenge because many existing adaptation strategies lack sustainability, particularly in the context of urban development. Sustainable urban development can be achieved through green initiatives, with green infrastructure (GI) practices playing an essential role in its implementation. Nevertheless, selecting one of the available GI practices might be challenging since their execution necessitates careful assessment of all relevant factors. To this end, we seek the aid of a fuzzy multi-criteria decision-making framework. This study considers six GI practices as alternatives, assessed based on environmental, economic, and social dimensions encompassing fourteen criteria. Spherical bipolar fuzzy sets (SBFS) are employed to represent the evaluation information. The significance of each criterion is established using the distance correlation-based criteria importance through inter-criteria correlation (D-CRITIC) technique, while the alternatives are ranked through the preference ranking on the basis of ideal-average distance (PROBID) approach. The proposed SBFS-based D-CRITIC-PROBID framework is a significant addition to the literature as a distinctive study on GI assessment. Further, the effectiveness and robustness of the findings are validated through comparison and sensitivity analyses.

Introduction

Rapid urbanization driven by increasing population and industrialization significantly impacts the natural ecosystem, primarily causing the degradation of green spaces as well as heightening environmental pollution. Currently, over half of the worldwide population dwells in urban and metropolitan regions, and predictions suggest that this figure could rise to 80% by 20501. Consequently, the usage of land has changed, with urban infrastructures taking the place of vegetation and unoccupied land2. This shift has an effect on the climate of urban regions, leading to abnormally high or low levels of humidity, precipitation, evapotranspiration, and air temperature3. The primary drivers of climate change include escalating greenhouse gas emissions, population expansion challenges, and consumerist lifestyle patterns4. It also has an impact on biodiversity and poses a risk to the ecosystem and human wellness. Growing interest has been shown in the possible role of green initiatives, which are frequently addressed with the idea of sustainable development, in finding solutions to these issues. The proper balance of economic, social, and environmental factors at the domestic, city, locality, neighbourhood, and household scales leads to sustainable development on a worldwide basis in urban regions. The Brundtland Commission, formerly termed the World Commission on Environment and Development, described sustainable development as “the development that satisfies current requirements without compromising the capacity of future generations to fulfil their own”5.

Nowadays, in the twenty-first century, green projects are gaining popularity among the people. In this regard, green infrastructure (GI) practices serve as a driving force in the pursuit of sustainable urban development (SUD)6. The underlying concept of GI is to preserve and restore the natural environment by developing eco-friendly infrastructure, prioritizing green spaces within urban and suburban regions, and possessing a vital role in preserving natural ecosystems while delivering significant benefits to people1. Some of the detrimental effects of urbanization on people and natural systems can be limited by the implementation of GI practices. It is regarded as an efficient approach in promoting urban sustainability that aids in the adaptation and mitigation of climate change, stormwater management, and heat mitigation. It has become more popular in urban areas because of its various benefits, such as reducing pollution and carbon emissions, enhancing the well-being of people, and improving the aesthetics of the environment. Although GI cannot completely replace natural landscapes, it is seen to be advantageous since it assists in protecting land- and water-dwelling organisms by providing habitats for various kinds of biota7. Furthermore, it facilitates water management by ensuring its effective utilization8.

Various GI practices are available to support SUD, providing practical solutions for handling environmental issues while enhancing urban resilience and liveability. Selecting an optimal choice among several GI practices can be difficult since their implementation requires systematic planning, financial sources, expert knowledge, and a comprehensive evaluation of all relevant parameters. This can be attained through the implementation of the multi-criteria decision-making (MCDM) technique. Among the several benefits of using MCDM techniques is the incorporation of varied criteria, which increases the dependability of the results. Numerous fields, including industrial, agricultural, biological, and energy systems, have made extensive usage of MCDM.

Aim of the study

This study aims to provide an effective framework that can be utilized by a variety of beneficiaries, including those in the public, private, and community sectors, to assess the optimal GI practice for SUD. In this context, we present an advanced decision-making methodology to determine the most sustainable GI practice. It depends on the integration of the Distance Correlation-based Criteria Importance Through Inter-criteria Correlation (D-CRITIC) method and the Preference Ranking on the Basis of Ideal-average Distance (PROBID) method under the Spherical Bipolar Fuzzy Sets (SBFS) environment. The developed SBFS-D-CRITIC-PROBID model can be implemented to solve various challenging MCDM problems pertaining to sustainable development.

Literature and motivation

Zadeh9 developed the notion of fuzzy sets to address the ambiguity and unpredictability that result from subjective opinions and data inconsistencies. Since then, several expansions and generalizations of fuzzy sets have been established by numerous researchers in the literature. Zhang10 initially presented bipolar-valued fuzzy sets (BFS) as a generalization of fuzzy sets with bipolarity (positive and negative), whose range of membership degree extends from [0,1] to [− 1,1]. Furthermore, Lee11 discussed certain basic BFS operations, and Lee et al.12 presented a comparison between the interval-valued fuzzy sets, IFS, and BFS. Recent applications of BFS decision-making include the assessment of high-risk coexisting tuberculosis disease in pregnant women using integrated COPRAS and TOPSIS approaches13 and the selection of unmanned aerial vehicle software using bipolar fuzzy aggregation operators14. The emergence of BFS gave rise to spherical fuzzy sets (SFS) presented by Kutlu Gündoğdu & Kahraman15, which combine the characteristics of PyFS and NS to offer a wider preference domain. One characteristic that makes SFS distinct is that each degree of membership, non-membership, and hesitancy is individually specified within the range [0, 1], along with its squared sum falling between 0 and 1. This allows investigators to express their hesitancies in greater depth. Recent applications of SFS decision-making include the assessment of environmental, social and governance performance of the global electronics industry employing SF-AHP and SF-WASPAS methodology16 and the selection of renewable energy sources in Turkey using integrated SF-DEMATEL, SF-ANP, and SF-VIKOR algorithms17. The concept of SBFS, which integrates the traits of SFS and BFS along with their properties, was first presented by Princy and Mohana18 and implemented in the MCDM application. The SBFS notion has been effectively implemented in the works of Çakır et al.19 and Deetae et al.20.

The MCDM weighting techniques are split into two categories, which include the subjective and objective methods. Subjective techniques need the decision-makers to provide some preliminary information before determining the weight. This information is typically based on their experience or expertise. Subjective approaches provide the benefit of incorporating information obtained from skilled decision-makers. But, due to the decision-makers’ prior beliefs, this information may occasionally favour particular criteria, providing biased outcomes. Additionally, when there are several criteria involved in the MCDM problem, the procedure of providing such information might get complicated. Objective techniques, in contrast to subjective ones, are devoid of any kind of preliminary data or opinion from the decision-makers. Instead of that, they depend only on the decision matrix to obtain the criteria weighting. These techniques are renowned for enhancing impartiality by removing any possible bias related to subjective assessment.

One of the most popular objective weighting techniques in the literature is CRITIC21, which considers both the contrast intensity and the degree of conflict between the evaluation criteria. This method utilizes the Pearson correlation to capture the conflicting relationships between criteria which may not always represent their actual relationships. As an example, two criteria having a Pearson correlation coefficient of 0 couldn’t be totally independent, since the Pearson correlation determines only the linear relationship between them. To resolve this limitation, Krishnan et al.22 formulated the D-CRITIC approach, an enhanced version of the CRITIC technique that captures the conflicting relationships between criteria by taking into account the concept of distance correlation, thereby providing a more credible set of criterion weights than the former one. Zhang and Wei23 applied the D-CRITIC approach under SFS to compute criteria weights in conjunction with the CoCoSo ranking technique for the location selection problem of electric vehicle charging stations. Maneengam24 used the D-CRITIC technique to assign the weight of each objective function along with the modified TOPSIS approach in order to solve the multimodal routing problem. Liu and Shen25 extended the D-CRITIC approach within the generalized hesitant fuzzy environment and combined it with the COPRAS approach to evaluate enterprise digital assets. Ghaemi-Zadeh and Eghbali-Zarch26 utilized the D-CRITIC technique, in combination with the fuzzy MULTI-MOORA ranking approach, to assess business strategy based on corporate performance and investor behaviour. Liu et al.27 investigated the location selection problem of emergency logistics center in Central China through the implementation of D-CRITIC-GRA methodology.

The ranking method selection is crucial in the assessment of the decision-making model. Various MCDM techniques exist in the literature, each having its unique calculation algorithm. Mufazzal and Muzakkir28 acknowledged the rank reversal behaviour, which is defined as the addition or removal of an alternative from the original dataset causing the ranking order to shift or reverse, as a drawback in several MCDM techniques. In order to overcome these issues, Wang et al.29 developed a new ranking algorithm called PROBID, which includes both the average and all possible ideal solutions, making it far more consistent than other well-known MCDM techniques. In this respect, Biswas et al.30 implemented the PROBID technique in combination with the FUCOM weight method under the picture fuzzy environment to rank the video conferencing platforms. Chatterjee et al.31 applied the PROBID approach and its simplified version, the sPROBID method, along with the SPC weighting technique in the problem of selecting a delivery drone. Hua et al.32 assessed companies according to their new quality productive forces using the IDOCRIW-PROBID methodology. Furthermore, the study carried out by Stević et al.33 examined the sensitivity assessments of seven distinct normalisation types in order to choose the small electric van using the PROBID, CODAS and SAW methodologies. Darko et al.34 employed the TODIM-based PROBID technique to assess electronic mental health platforms under probabilistic reliable linguistic term sets. The criterion weights were measured using the CRTITC approach.

Recent studies have demonstrated the growing applicability of advanced fuzzy MCDM methods across diverse decision-making domains. For instance, the CRITIC–TOPSIS methodology has been employed for sustainable medical waste management under an f, g, h - fractional fuzzy set environment35. Similarly, the CRITIC–WASPAS approach has been applied to sustainable energy planning using f, g, h - fractional fuzzy sets36. In another innovative direction, a TOPSIS-based framework has been developed for optimal gate security system selection by integrating quantum mechanics principles into spherical fuzzy sets37. Additionally, the BWM–CoCoSo methodology has been utilized in supply chain management by incorporating Z-number theory38. Likewise, the SWARA–WASPAS approach using fuzzy numbers39 and the SWARA–MARCOS technique using grey numbers40 have both been applied in the domain of supply chain.

Alongside MCDM-driven assessments, certain studies conceptualize urban agriculture as a form of GI, highlighting the benefits pertinent to stormwater management, carbon mitigation, food security, and environmental and social sustainability41,42. By emphasizing land-use planning and ecologically friendly strategies, these studies broaden the conceptual scope of GI beyond engineered solutions. Furthermore, planning-focused research highlights the integration of GI within spatial planning, land-use management, and transportation systems to improve urban resilience and sustainability43,44. These studies indicate that the implementation of GI is influenced not just by environmental performance but also by planning policies and governance frameworks.

State of the art in GI decision-making

There are very few studies in the literature that incorporate MCDM in the evaluation of GI. For instance, Lin et al.1 employed a modified VIKOR approach to evaluate six typical GIs for environmental sustainability, in which the DEMATEL-based ANP technique was utilized to figure out the relative importance of the ten considered criteria, which were grouped under four dimensions. Arya and Kumar45 assessed five distinct GIs for sustainable stormwater management, both independently and in combination, based on five criteria encompassing fourteen sub-criteria. The criterion weights were generated using the AHP approach, and the alternatives were ranked employing the TOPSIS technique. Additionally, using the DEMATEL-based ANP approach, Khoshnava et al.6 evaluated six GI criteria in relation to the use of the green economy in the framework of sustainable development.

However, there is yet no expansion of the D-CRITIC technique into the SBFS environment. Decision-makers might find this expansion beneficial in assessing the significance of assessment criteria. The PROBID method has not been integrated yet into the D-CRITIC approach under the SBFS environment, which allows the decision-makers to evaluate the alternatives and identify the most effective solution. Nevertheless, there is still no literature on the application of GI in the context of SUD using an enhanced fuzzy decision model. This requirement serves as the driving force behind the development of this study.

Research gaps and contributions

The following are the highlighted research gaps: The D-CRITIC approach has not been developed in the SBFS environment in any prior study. The PROBID technique’s implementation in the SBFS setting has not yet been examined. No prior research has integrated the D-CRITIC and PROBID approaches into a single framework for the assessment of GI selection.

The following are the study’s contributions:This study proposes an enhanced MCDM framework based on the combination of the D-CRITIC and PROBID techniques to select the appropriate GI practice in the context of SUD. In the combined D-CRITIC-PROBID methodology, expert assessment information is represented utilizing SBFS to eliminate imprecision and ambiguity. To our best knowledge, this appears to be the first study to integrate the D-CRITIC and PROBID methods under the SBFS environment.It provides an evaluation framework with fourteen criteria categorized into environmental, economic and social dimensions. The SBFS-based D-CRITIC approach was developed to establish the objective weights of the decision-making criteria for sustainable GI selection, which provides a reliable criterion weighting system for decision-makers.Then, the SBFS-based PROBID method was formulated to evaluate six GI practices and determine the most optimal solution. This presents a dependable evaluation of GI practices, which is essential for examining its effectiveness.The proposed SBFS-based D-CRITIC-PROBID model shall facilitate the researchers in solving various complex problems. It is anticipated to make a substantial contribution to the field of MCDM as a unique research on GI evaluation.The following sections structure the remainder of the article: Section 2 provides the fundamental definitions related to SBFS. Section 3 details the D-CRITIC and PROBID methodologies. The case study of GI selection is provided in Section 4. Section 5 discusses the results of the proposed model along with comparative and sensitivity analyses, and the concluding comments are given in Section 6.

This study proposes an enhanced MCDM framework based on the combination of the D-CRITIC and PROBID techniques to select the appropriate GI practice in the context of SUD. In the combined D-CRITIC-PROBID methodology, expert assessment information is represented utilizing SBFS to eliminate imprecision and ambiguity. To our best knowledge, this appears to be the first study to integrate the D-CRITIC and PROBID methods under the SBFS environment.

Definition 4

_{i=1}^{n}(1-(\eta ^{+}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}_i})^2)^{\varpi _i}\bigg ]^{1/2},\bigg [\prod _{i=1}^{n}(1-(\eta ^{+}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}_i})^2)^{\varpi _i}-\prod _{i=1}^{n}(1-(\eta ^{-}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}_i})^2)^{\varpi _i}\bigg ]^{1/2}\bigg \} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Definition 5

18 The score function of a SBFS, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}=( \zeta ^{+}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}},\eta ^{+}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}},\delta ^{+}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}}, \zeta ^{-}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}},\eta ^{-}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}},\delta ^{-}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}})$$\end{document} is determined by:5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Score(\widetilde{\mathbb {S}})=\frac{1}{2}\big [(\zeta ^{+}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}}-\delta ^{+}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}})^2-(\eta ^{+}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {R}}}-\delta ^{+}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}})^2+(\zeta ^{-}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}}-\delta ^{-}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}})^2-(\eta ^{-}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}}-\delta ^{-}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}})^2\big ] \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Methodology

The proposed methodology commences with a decision matrix which denotes the performance of different alternatives based on predetermined criteria, as shown in Eq. 6. The set of alternatives to be ranked is represented as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_u=\mathfrak {B}_1, \mathfrak {B}_2, \mathfrak {B}_3, \cdots , \mathfrak {B}_g$$\end{document}, while the set of criteria that are employed to rank the alternatives is represented as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}_v=\mathfrak {C}_1, \mathfrak {C}_2, \mathfrak {C}_3, \cdots , \mathfrak {C}_h$$\end{document}. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{gh}$$\end{document} is the performance of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u^{th}$$\end{document} alternative on the basis of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v^{th}$$\end{document} criterion.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Score(\widetilde{\mathbb {S}})=\frac{1}{2}\big [(\zeta ^{+}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}}-\delta ^{+}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}})^2-(\eta ^{+}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {R}}}-\delta ^{+}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}})^2+(\zeta ^{-}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}}-\delta ^{-}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}})^2-(\eta ^{-}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}}-\delta ^{-}_{\widetilde{\mathbb {S}}})^2\big ] \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Each alternative’s performance was gathered using SBFS, after which the D-CRITIC technique was utilized to evaluate the relative importance of the criteria, and the PROBID approach was utilized to establish the optimal choice. A BFS captures the dual nature of human perception and cognition. It is appropriate to handle information containing a property, as well as its counter-property. SFS has the freedom in uncertainty representation, allowing membership, non-membership, and hesitancy to coexist with greater flexibility46. Combining these two sets serves as an efficient measure for eliminating ambiguity in experts’ recommendations for making decisions and enhancing the identification of information uncertainties. It better articulates the vagueness and uncertainty of the evaluation system. The added value of bipolarity in the selection of GI practice encompasses both the benefits and drawbacks of a GI alternative simultaneously. The negative membership is essential since the GI alternatives often exhibit explicit drawbacks that cannot be captured as a mere absence of benefit.

D-CRITIC method

The D-CRITIC technique incorporates the concept of distance correlation into the conventional CRITIC approach, capturing both linear and nonlinear relations between criteria. Compared to the original CRITIC technique, it can provide more efficient standard weights and more accurately replicate the conflict connection between criteria. The existence of both beneficial (BF) and non-beneficial (NBF) criteria, as well as negative elements in the decision matrix, constitutes the basis for using this approach. In Step 3, the D-CRITIC technique employs distance correlation, whereas the original CRITIC method utilizes Pearson correlation, which is the main difference between the two approaches. PROBID method

The fundamental concept behind the PROBID approach is to comprehensively evaluate both the average and all possible ideal solutions. It typically combines the benefits of the EDAS and TOPSIS approaches and is not prone to rank reversal. Compared to other well-known MCDM techniques, PROBID appears to be consistent due to its comprehensive covering of ideal solutions. It can provide more reliable solutions for MCDM problems, especially those with larger datasets and high dimensions.Step 1 Compute the normalized decision matrix by means of vector normalization using Eq. (15). 15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \vec {\theta }_{uv}=\frac{\theta _{uv}}{\sqrt{\sum _{u=1}^{g}\theta ^2_{uv}}} \hspace{1cm} (u=1,2,\cdots ,g; \hspace{0.1cm} v=1,2,\cdots ,h), \end{aligned}$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vec {\theta }_{uv}$$\end{document} is the normalized element of the actual element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _{uv}$$\end{document}.Step 2 Establish the weighted normalized decision matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\lambda _{uv})$$\end{document} utilizing Eq. (16). 16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda _{uv}=\vec {\theta }_{uv} \times \Upsilon _v \hspace{1cm} (u=1,2,\cdots ,g; \hspace{0.1cm} v=1,2,\cdots ,h), \end{aligned}$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vec {\theta }_{uv}$$\end{document} is the normalized value. Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Upsilon _v$$\end{document} denotes the objective weight of the criteria acquired from the D-CRITIC approach.Step 3 Obtain the most positive ideal solution (PIS) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\breve{\mathcal {A}}_1)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^{nd}$$\end{document} PIS \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\breve{\mathcal {A}}_2)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^{rd}$$\end{document} PIS \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\breve{\mathcal {A}}_3)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\cdots$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g^{th}$$\end{document} PIS \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\breve{\mathcal {A}}_g)$$\end{document} [i.e., the most negative ideal solution (NIS)] by Eq. (17). 17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} The distance of the ideal solutions is calculated using Eq. (20). 20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{u(k)}=\sqrt{\sum _{v=1}^{h}(\lambda _{uv}-\lambda _{(k)v})^2} \hspace{1cm} (u=1,2,\cdots ,g; \hspace{0.1cm} k=1,2,\cdots ,g). \end{aligned}$$\end{document} In the same way, the distance of the average solution is computed utilizing Eq. (21). 21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{u(avg)}=\sqrt{\sum _{v=1}^{h}(\lambda _{uv}-\bar{\lambda }_v)^2} \hspace{1cm} (u=1,2,\cdots ,g). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Step 5 Calculate the overall positive-ideal distance (PID) by Eq. (22), which basically represents the weighted sum distance of one solution to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1^{st}$$\end{document} half of the ideal solutions. 22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{u(pos-ideal)}={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \sum _{k=1}^{(g+1)/2}\frac{1}{k}\mathcal {E}_{u(k)} & (u=1,2,\cdots ,g)\hspace{0.1cm} when \hspace{0.1cm} g \hspace{0.1cm} is \hspace{0.1cm} an \hspace{0.1cm} odd \hspace{0.1cm} number \\ \sum _{k=1}^{g/2}\frac{1}{k}\mathcal {E}_{u(k)} & (u=1,2,\cdots ,g)\hspace{0.1cm} when \hspace{0.1cm} g \hspace{0.1cm} is \hspace{0.1cm} an \hspace{0.1cm} even \hspace{0.1cm} number \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document} In this case, weight decreases with the ideal solution number (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=1,2,3,\cdots$$\end{document}). Likewise, calculate the overall negative-ideal distance (NID) by Eq. (23), which represents the weighted sum distance of one solution to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^{nd}$$\end{document} half of the ideal solutions. 23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{u(neg-ideal)}={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \sum _{k=(g+1)/2}^{g}\frac{1}{g-k+1}\mathcal {E}_{u(k)} & (u=1,2,\cdots ,g)\hspace{0.1cm} when \hspace{0.1cm} g \hspace{0.1cm} is \hspace{0.1cm} an \hspace{0.1cm} odd \hspace{0.1cm} number \\ \sum _{k=g/2+1}^{g}\frac{1}{g-k+1}\mathcal {E}_{u(k)} & (u=1,2,\cdots ,g)\hspace{0.1cm} when \hspace{0.1cm} g \hspace{0.1cm} is \hspace{0.1cm} an \hspace{0.1cm} even \hspace{0.1cm} number \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document} In this case, weight increases with the ideal solution number (i.e., k increasing to m).Step 6 Using Eq. (24), calculate the ratio (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _u$$\end{document}) of the overall PID to the overall NID. 24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi _u=\frac{\mathcal {E}_{u(pos-ideal)}}{\mathcal {E}_{u(neg-ideal)}} \hspace{1cm} (u=1,2,\cdots ,g). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Step 7 Determine the performance score (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _u$$\end{document}) of each solution using Eq. (25). 25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Gamma _u=\frac{1}{1+\Phi _{u}^2}+\mathcal {E}_{u(avg)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document} It is seen from Eq. (24) that a smaller value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _u$$\end{document} indicates that the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u^{th}$$\end{document} solution is nearer to PIS, which results in a greater value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _u$$\end{document}. On the other hand, a greater \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _u$$\end{document} value indicates that the solution is nearer to NIS, which results in a smaller \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _u$$\end{document} value. The alternative that has a higher \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _u$$\end{document} value turns out to be the ideal choice.An outline of the proposed research framework is depicted in Fig. 1.Fig. 1Overall framework of the proposed work.

Step 4 Determine the Euclidean distance between each solution and each of the g ideal solutions along with the average solution. The distance of the ideal solutions is calculated using Eq. (20). 20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{u(k)}=\sqrt{\sum _{v=1}^{h}(\lambda _{uv}-\lambda _{(k)v})^2} \hspace{1cm} (u=1,2,\cdots ,g; \hspace{0.1cm} k=1,2,\cdots ,g). \end{aligned}$$\end{document} In the same way, the distance of the average solution is computed utilizing Eq. (21). 21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{u(avg)}=\sqrt{\sum _{v=1}^{h}(\lambda _{uv}-\bar{\lambda }_v)^2} \hspace{1cm} (u=1,2,\cdots ,g). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Step 4 Determine the Euclidean distance between each solution and each of the g ideal solutions along with the average solution. The distance of the ideal solutions is calculated using Eq. (20). 20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{u(k)}=\sqrt{\sum _{v=1}^{h}(\lambda _{uv}-\lambda _{(k)v})^2} \hspace{1cm} (u=1,2,\cdots ,g; \hspace{0.1cm} k=1,2,\cdots ,g). \end{aligned}$$\end{document} In the same way, the distance of the average solution is computed utilizing Eq. (21). 21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{u(avg)}=\sqrt{\sum _{v=1}^{h}(\lambda _{uv}-\bar{\lambda }_v)^2} \hspace{1cm} (u=1,2,\cdots ,g). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Step 5 Calculate the overall positive-ideal distance (PID) by Eq. (22), which basically represents the weighted sum distance of one solution to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1^{st}$$\end{document} half of the ideal solutions. 22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{u(pos-ideal)}={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \sum _{k=1}^{(g+1)/2}\frac{1}{k}\mathcal {E}_{u(k)} & (u=1,2,\cdots ,g)\hspace{0.1cm} when \hspace{0.1cm} g \hspace{0.1cm} is \hspace{0.1cm} an \hspace{0.1cm} odd \hspace{0.1cm} number \\ \sum _{k=1}^{g/2}\frac{1}{k}\mathcal {E}_{u(k)} & (u=1,2,\cdots ,g)\hspace{0.1cm} when \hspace{0.1cm} g \hspace{0.1cm} is \hspace{0.1cm} an \hspace{0.1cm} even \hspace{0.1cm} number \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document} In this case, weight decreases with the ideal solution number (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=1,2,3,\cdots$$\end{document}). Likewise, calculate the overall negative-ideal distance (NID) by Eq. (23), which represents the weighted sum distance of one solution to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^{nd}$$\end{document} half of the ideal solutions. 23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{u(neg-ideal)}={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \sum _{k=(g+1)/2}^{g}\frac{1}{g-k+1}\mathcal {E}_{u(k)} & (u=1,2,\cdots ,g)\hspace{0.1cm} when \hspace{0.1cm} g \hspace{0.1cm} is \hspace{0.1cm} an \hspace{0.1cm} odd \hspace{0.1cm} number \\ \sum _{k=g/2+1}^{g}\frac{1}{g-k+1}\mathcal {E}_{u(k)} & (u=1,2,\cdots ,g)\hspace{0.1cm} when \hspace{0.1cm} g \hspace{0.1cm} is \hspace{0.1cm} an \hspace{0.1cm} even \hspace{0.1cm} number \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document} In this case, weight increases with the ideal solution number (i.e., k increasing to m).

Step 5 Calculate the overall positive-ideal distance (PID) by Eq. (22), which basically represents the weighted sum distance of one solution to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1^{st}$$\end{document} half of the ideal solutions. 22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{u(pos-ideal)}={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \sum _{k=1}^{(g+1)/2}\frac{1}{k}\mathcal {E}_{u(k)} & (u=1,2,\cdots ,g)\hspace{0.1cm} when \hspace{0.1cm} g \hspace{0.1cm} is \hspace{0.1cm} an \hspace{0.1cm} odd \hspace{0.1cm} number \\ \sum _{k=1}^{g/2}\frac{1}{k}\mathcal {E}_{u(k)} & (u=1,2,\cdots ,g)\hspace{0.1cm} when \hspace{0.1cm} g \hspace{0.1cm} is \hspace{0.1cm} an \hspace{0.1cm} even \hspace{0.1cm} number \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document} In this case, weight decreases with the ideal solution number (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=1,2,3,\cdots$$\end{document}). Likewise, calculate the overall negative-ideal distance (NID) by Eq. (23), which represents the weighted sum distance of one solution to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^{nd}$$\end{document} half of the ideal solutions. 23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{u(neg-ideal)}={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \sum _{k=(g+1)/2}^{g}\frac{1}{g-k+1}\mathcal {E}_{u(k)} & (u=1,2,\cdots ,g)\hspace{0.1cm} when \hspace{0.1cm} g \hspace{0.1cm} is \hspace{0.1cm} an \hspace{0.1cm} odd \hspace{0.1cm} number \\ \sum _{k=g/2+1}^{g}\frac{1}{g-k+1}\mathcal {E}_{u(k)} & (u=1,2,\cdots ,g)\hspace{0.1cm} when \hspace{0.1cm} g \hspace{0.1cm} is \hspace{0.1cm} an \hspace{0.1cm} even \hspace{0.1cm} number \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document} In this case, weight increases with the ideal solution number (i.e., k increasing to m).

Step 6 Using Eq. (24), calculate the ratio (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _u$$\end{document}) of the overall PID to the overall NID. 24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi _u=\frac{\mathcal {E}_{u(pos-ideal)}}{\mathcal {E}_{u(neg-ideal)}} \hspace{1cm} (u=1,2,\cdots ,g). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Step 6 Using Eq. (24), calculate the ratio (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _u$$\end{document}) of the overall PID to the overall NID. 24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi _u=\frac{\mathcal {E}_{u(pos-ideal)}}{\mathcal {E}_{u(neg-ideal)}} \hspace{1cm} (u=1,2,\cdots ,g). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Step 7 Determine the performance score (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _u$$\end{document}) of each solution using Eq. (25). 25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Gamma _u=\frac{1}{1+\Phi _{u}^2}+\mathcal {E}_{u(avg)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document} It is seen from Eq. (24) that a smaller value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _u$$\end{document} indicates that the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u^{th}$$\end{document} solution is nearer to PIS, which results in a greater value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _u$$\end{document}. On the other hand, a greater \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _u$$\end{document} value indicates that the solution is nearer to NIS, which results in a smaller \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _u$$\end{document} value. The alternative that has a higher \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _u$$\end{document} value turns out to be the ideal choice.

Step 7 Determine the performance score (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _u$$\end{document}) of each solution using Eq. (25). 25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Gamma _u=\frac{1}{1+\Phi _{u}^2}+\mathcal {E}_{u(avg)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document} It is seen from Eq. (24) that a smaller value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _u$$\end{document} indicates that the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u^{th}$$\end{document} solution is nearer to PIS, which results in a greater value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _u$$\end{document}. On the other hand, a greater \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _u$$\end{document} value indicates that the solution is nearer to NIS, which results in a smaller \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _u$$\end{document} value. The alternative that has a higher \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _u$$\end{document} value turns out to be the ideal choice.

Overall framework of the proposed work.

Case study

Urban regions need to adapt to the intricacies brought about by economic expansion and climate change, in addition to various other challenges. The necessity of adapting to these changes is widely acknowledged across global levels. However, most of the existing adaptation strategies fail to be sustainable, making sustainable development a significant task. GI practices have gained popularity recently as an advantageous and adapted approach to sustainable development. Effective planning of GI might improve a region’s resilience and long-term sustainability. In order to promote SUD, this study examines six potential GI practices, namely green roofs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_1)$$\end{document}, rain gardens \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_2)$$\end{document}, urban forests \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_3)$$\end{document}, permeable pavements \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_4)$$\end{document}, grass swales \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_5)$$\end{document}, and green walls \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_6)$$\end{document}, as alternatives. The following is a description of each of these alternatives. (i)Green roofs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_1)$$\end{document} Green roofs, commonly referred to as vegetated or living roofs, are rooftops that are covered entirely or in part by vegetation, which might include grasses, herbs, shrubs, plants, and even tiny trees integrated with the growing medium and planted over a waterproof layer. Other layers, like irrigation and drainage networks, and even a root barrier, could also be included.(ii)Rain gardens \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_2)$$\end{document}: Rain gardens are landscaped with different kinds of vegetation in the appropriate soil, such as shrubs, grass, and floral perennials, in a shallow depression that collects rainwater from rooftops, sidewalks, roadways, and streets and lets it seep into the ground, reducing the risk of flood and water pollution. It may also incorporate filtration media, mulching layers, and possibly underdrains.(iii)Urban forests \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_3)$$\end{document}Urban forests comprise groups of trees and other types of woody vegetation that grow in and around residential areas located within the urban and suburban regions, which may include trees in recreational spaces, along streets, and in neglected or unused areas. Both private and public entities may possess urban forests.(iv)Permeable pavements \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_4)$$\end{document}Permeable pavements are surfaces composed of either a porous substance that allows water to pass through it or of nonporous blocks separated by spaces that enable water to pass between those openings, permitting the percolation and infiltration of the rainwater into the aggregate layers or soil beneath the pavement. It is widely employed on roadways, sidewalks and parking lots.(v)Grass swales \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_5)$$\end{document}Grass swales, a form of vegetated swales, are graded and designed landscape structures that are lined with grass possessing a trapezoidal or parabolic cross-section. They are typically linear, shallow, and slightly sloped open channels. They are commonly utilised to reduce runoff in residential areas, around buildings, and along roadways and parking lots.(vi)Green walls \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_6)$$\end{document}Green walls, also referred to as vertical gardens, are structures having the whole or portions of their walls coated with vegetation on a growing medium that is placed vertically. They usually incorporate a range of plant species with an arrangement that enables vertical plant growth. Both the interior and exterior of the structures may have green walls installed in different sizes.The three fundamental aspects of sustainability, viz., environmental, economic and social, serve as the foundation of sustainable development. Therefore, the GI practice for SUD can also be evaluated using these three factors. Despite the fact that GI may be beneficial in numerous situations, it is typically employed for certain purposes, involving flood prevention, water and air treatment, or enhancing local attractiveness. Thus, the criteria need to consider these factors to better comprehend the relationships between social, economic, and environmental aspects. Following a literary survey, fourteen evaluation criteria were selected for this study, which were grouped under three dimensions utilized to assess the six alternatives considered. The featured GI practices are widely known and well suited for the implementation of the local urban environment. Table 1 provides a description of these criteria. The + and − signs stand for the BF and NBF criteria, respectively. The alternatives and criteria are illustrated graphically in Fig. 2.Table 1List of the evaluation criteria.DimensionCriteriaDescriptionEnvironmental (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} (i)Green roofs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_1)$$\end{document} Green roofs, commonly referred to as vegetated or living roofs, are rooftops that are covered entirely or in part by vegetation, which might include grasses, herbs, shrubs, plants, and even tiny trees integrated with the growing medium and planted over a waterproof layer. Other layers, like irrigation and drainage networks, and even a root barrier, could also be included.

Green roofs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_1)$$\end{document} Green roofs, commonly referred to as vegetated or living roofs, are rooftops that are covered entirely or in part by vegetation, which might include grasses, herbs, shrubs, plants, and even tiny trees integrated with the growing medium and planted over a waterproof layer. Other layers, like irrigation and drainage networks, and even a root barrier, could also be included.

(ii)Rain gardens \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_2)$$\end{document}: Rain gardens are landscaped with different kinds of vegetation in the appropriate soil, such as shrubs, grass, and floral perennials, in a shallow depression that collects rainwater from rooftops, sidewalks, roadways, and streets and lets it seep into the ground, reducing the risk of flood and water pollution. It may also incorporate filtration media, mulching layers, and possibly underdrains.

Rain gardens \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_2)$$\end{document}: Rain gardens are landscaped with different kinds of vegetation in the appropriate soil, such as shrubs, grass, and floral perennials, in a shallow depression that collects rainwater from rooftops, sidewalks, roadways, and streets and lets it seep into the ground, reducing the risk of flood and water pollution. It may also incorporate filtration media, mulching layers, and possibly underdrains.

(iii)Urban forests \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_3)$$\end{document}Urban forests comprise groups of trees and other types of woody vegetation that grow in and around residential areas located within the urban and suburban regions, which may include trees in recreational spaces, along streets, and in neglected or unused areas. Both private and public entities may possess urban forests.

Urban forests \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_3)$$\end{document}Urban forests comprise groups of trees and other types of woody vegetation that grow in and around residential areas located within the urban and suburban regions, which may include trees in recreational spaces, along streets, and in neglected or unused areas. Both private and public entities may possess urban forests.

(iv)Permeable pavements \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_4)$$\end{document}Permeable pavements are surfaces composed of either a porous substance that allows water to pass through it or of nonporous blocks separated by spaces that enable water to pass between those openings, permitting the percolation and infiltration of the rainwater into the aggregate layers or soil beneath the pavement. It is widely employed on roadways, sidewalks and parking lots.

Permeable pavements \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_4)$$\end{document}Permeable pavements are surfaces composed of either a porous substance that allows water to pass through it or of nonporous blocks separated by spaces that enable water to pass between those openings, permitting the percolation and infiltration of the rainwater into the aggregate layers or soil beneath the pavement. It is widely employed on roadways, sidewalks and parking lots.

(v)Grass swales \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_5)$$\end{document}Grass swales, a form of vegetated swales, are graded and designed landscape structures that are lined with grass possessing a trapezoidal or parabolic cross-section. They are typically linear, shallow, and slightly sloped open channels. They are commonly utilised to reduce runoff in residential areas, around buildings, and along roadways and parking lots.

Grass swales \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_5)$$\end{document}Grass swales, a form of vegetated swales, are graded and designed landscape structures that are lined with grass possessing a trapezoidal or parabolic cross-section. They are typically linear, shallow, and slightly sloped open channels. They are commonly utilised to reduce runoff in residential areas, around buildings, and along roadways and parking lots.

(vi)Green walls \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_6)$$\end{document}Green walls, also referred to as vertical gardens, are structures having the whole or portions of their walls coated with vegetation on a growing medium that is placed vertically. They usually incorporate a range of plant species with an arrangement that enables vertical plant growth. Both the interior and exterior of the structures may have green walls installed in different sizes.

Green walls \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_6)$$\end{document}Green walls, also referred to as vertical gardens, are structures having the whole or portions of their walls coated with vegetation on a growing medium that is placed vertically. They usually incorporate a range of plant species with an arrangement that enables vertical plant growth. Both the interior and exterior of the structures may have green walls installed in different sizes.

Furthermore, the implementation of GI practice in an urban setting cannot be determined by a single individual. Hence, forming a panel of experts is essential to guarantee a sustainable GI practice. Therefore, this study gathered insights from an environmental engineer (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {X}_1$$\end{document}), a landscape architect (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {X}_2$$\end{document}), and an urban planner (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {X}_3$$\end{document}), who provided independent evaluations on the performance of GI practices based on the criteria under consideration, which were subsequently synthesized into a single decision matrix using the SBFS aggregation operator to handle conflicting opinions. Environmental engineers ensure that a GI is technically efficient. Landscape architects assist in presenting them in an eco-friendly and aesthetic manner, while urban planners focus on the strategic positioning of the GI. Their contributions are essential to the planning, designing, and implementation of GI in order to ensure its sustainability and functionality. SBFS are adaptive to these circumstances by offering a more subtle and flexible representation useful for decision-making purposes. At this point, the PROBID methodology can be effectively utilized to assess alternatives based on a number of criteria represented by SBFS, enabling a systematic approach. Consequently, SBF-PROBID is suggested to order the alternatives in accordance with expert’s recommendations.Fig. 2Graphical illustration of alternatives and criteria.

Graphical illustration of alternatives and criteria.

Results and discussion

According to the definition of SBFS, the linguistic scale is determined and displayed in Table 2. The panel of experts assessed the performance of each alternative in relation to every criterion, and the evaluation values are represented as linguistic terms in Table 3. Every expert must evaluate their recommendation on the basis of SBFS. The experts’ weight, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {X}$$\end{document} = (0.4, 0.35, 0.25), is determined by their requirements for the particular problem. The experts’ respective competence and decision-making significance in GI planning are reflected in the weights assigned to them, with greater emphasis focused on technical and implementation-oriented experience. The SBWGM operator is employed, and the aggregated SBF decision matrix is developed using Eq. (4). Then, the SBF score matrix is determined by employing Eq. (5), which is presented in Table 4. This score matrix serves as the decision-making matrix for subsequent steps.Table 2Linguistic scale for SBFS.Linguistic termAbbreviationSBFNVery extremely effectiveVEE(0.92, 0.08, 0.08, − 0.13, − \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}_{11}$$\end{document} = 0.414, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}_{12}$$\end{document} = 0.362, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}_{13}$$\end{document} = 0.371, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}_{14}$$\end{document} = 0.438. Next, in order to obtain the distance correlation between each pair of criteria, the Euclidean distance matrix is created utilizing Eq. (9), and the double-centred matrix is then determined using Eq. (10). High distance correlation values reflect strong interdependence among criteria, implying that policy interventions affecting one criterion are likely to influence others simultaneously, which is valuable for integrated and coordinated decision-making. Following that, Eq. (11) is used to calculate the distance covariance of the criterion. Lastly, the distance correlation is determined using Eq. (12), and Table 5 shows their outcomes. Subsequently, the information content of each criterion is determined in Table 6 employing Eq. (13). Finally, the objective weights of each criterion, which were calculated using Eq. (14) have been presented graphically in Fig. 3. It is evident from the results that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}_1$$\end{document}, viz., stormwater management, has the highest relative importance, indicating the effective control of flooding as the most indispensable factor, followed by public recognition (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}_{14}$$\end{document}) and lifespan (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}_9$$\end{document}), emphasizing the significance of social acceptance and longevity. A strong emphasis is given to water purification (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}_5$$\end{document}) and biodiversity enhancement (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}_3$$\end{document}), which highlights the importance of environmental benefits. Cost (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}_7$$\end{document}), ease of installation (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}_{10}$$\end{document}), heat mitigation (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}_2$$\end{document}), and health and wellness (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}_{12}$$\end{document}) are fairly taken into consideration, ensuring economic viability, technical feasibility and social well-being. Space efficiency (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}_8$$\end{document}) and energy efficiency (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}_6$$\end{document}) obtain the least relative importance, making them essential for sustainability but not primary drivers.
