The information content of each criterion.

Fig. 3Graphical representation of criteria weights.

Graphical representation of criteria weights.

Following this, the PROBID approach is employed to evaluate the alternatives and determine the optimal GI practice. The initial procedure is to normalize the decision matrix by means of vector normalization using Eq. (15). To align with the distinct objectives of the considered approaches, different normalization techniques have been utilized. Linear normalization in D-CRITIC retains variability and inter-criteria relationships essential for reliable weight estimation, whereas vector normalization in PROBID assures impartial distance-based ranking. Without compromising the stability of the findings, this distinction preserves methodological consistency. Then, employing the weights obtained from the D-CRITIC approach, Eq. (16) yields the weighted normalized decision matrix. Next, Table 7 provides the ideal and average solutions derived from Eqs. (17) and (18). Eqs. (20) and (21) are used to calculate the Euclidean distances \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {E}_{u(k)})$$\end{document} of each solution to the ideal solutions and average solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {E}_{u(avg)})$$\end{document} which is presented in Table 8. Table 9 displays the overall PID \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {E}_{u(pos-ideal)})$$\end{document}, overall NID \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {E}_{u(neg-ideal)})$$\end{document}, their ratio \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Phi _u)$$\end{document}, and performance score \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Gamma _u)$$\end{document} of each of the GI practices, obtained using Eqs. (22–25). The alternatives are sorted based on the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _u$$\end{document} value in decreasing order. Higher the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _u$$\end{document} value, the better is the alternative. The ranking results of the alternatives are shown graphically in Fig. 4. The findings show that rain gardens (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_2$$\end{document}) rank first, followed by urban forests (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_3$$\end{document}), green roofs (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_1$$\end{document}), permeable pavements (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_4$$\end{document}), green walls (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_6$$\end{document}) and grass swales (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_5$$\end{document}). Marginal score differences between closely ranked alternatives, such as between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_2$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_3$$\end{document} (0.031) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_3$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_1$$\end{document} (0.039), indicate proximity to the same decision boundary and are unlikely to be practically meaningful for planners and policymakers. These options should therefore be considered using contextual factors (criteria) rather than rank alone. Sensitivity analysis further shows that excluding socially driven criteria leads only to marginal shifts among intermediate alternatives, while the optimal option remains unchanged.Table 7Ideal and Interpretation of the results

Rain gardens are effective in managing stormwater by absorbing runoff, minimizing the risk of floods and erosion. This helps in replenishing groundwater and mitigating heat to enhance climate resilience. They also improve the quality of the water by filtering the contaminants from the runoff before it reaches the groundwater or nearby water bodies. By providing habitat for a range of native plants, birds, and other species, they raise the likelihood of urban biodiversity. Rain gardens are relatively easy to install in a variety of urban locations, such as parking lots, roadways, and residential areas. They utilize colourful and low-maintenance plants that enhance the aesthetics of the landscapes while also offering the advantage of low installation and maintenance costs. It is one of the widely accepted GI practices and poses a minimal level of risk to society. Thus, with their significant beneficial impacts on the environment, economy and society, rain gardens lead the list, making them an ideal GI practice. Urban forests come next due to their broader environmental and social benefits, followed by green roofs for their multifunctional use of space. Permeable pavements provide good infiltration but less ecological benefits, while green walls and grass swales are more limited by maintenance needs and space constraints, placing them lower in the ranking. By identifying effectively functioning alternatives, the proposed model promotes targeted investments in GI to resolve regionally relevant concerns such as urban floods, heat reduction, and land utilization. From the viewpoint of policy, these findings can help planning authorities and local governments in integrating GI projects with sustainability objectives, plans for adapting to climate change, and urban development regulations. The study enhances the fuzzy MCDM framework by integrating uncertainty, diverse criteria, and expert judgement into a cohesive structure for GI evaluation. The findings support real-world urban sustainability policies by offering a structured, decision-support prioritization of GI practices that balances environmental, economic and social objectives. By explicitly incorporating trade-offs and expert uncertainty, the paradigm assists policymakers in identifying viable and efficient GI options.

Spatial, land-use, and transportation dimensions of GI

The proposed fuzzy MCDM model positions GI selection within the broader context of spatial planning, land-use management, and transportation systems by serving as an adaptable decision-making tool for integrated urban development. Building-integrated GI like green roofs and green walls enable compact development in alignment with spatial planning goals, while surface-based GI like rain gardens, urban forests, and grass swales are associated with land-use zoning and public spaces. Transportation-orientated GI, such as permeable pavements and grass swales, highlights the integration of GI within transport corridors. GI is strongly related to transportation planning, since these practices are commonly implemented into roadways, sidewalks and parking lots to improve walkability and reduce runoff, heat waves, air pollution, and noise. Environmental performance, land-use viability, and societal balance factors are also reflected in the evaluation criteria. By accommodating expert-based fuzzy evaluation, the framework enables these planning elements to be methodically represented in the assessment and selection of GI, promoting more cohesive and SUD strategies.

Leapfrogging towards GI entails the direct adoption of nature-based solutions, unlike incremental initiatives that integrate GI into pre-existing urban systems. The evaluated GI practices are ideal for leapfrogging situations, as they can be incorporated into buildings, streets, and open areas at early phases of urban development. By enabling criterion weights and expert judgements to be modified in accordance with various planning priorities, the suggested fuzzy MCDM framework supports transformative planning scenarios alongside incremental urban development strategies. The expert-based fuzzy assessment can be enhanced by spatially grounded case studies, which provide actual geographical information to enable more robust weighting and improve criterion selection. Expert opinions can be supported by observed differences in GI performance among various urban designs, land-use patterns, and transportation systems. Such insights can guide future extensions of the model towards GIS-integrated decision-making for context-sensitive GI planning.

Comparative analysis

To illustrate the consistency and efficacy of the proposed method, a comparative assessment was carried out based on the same dataset utilized by the proposed method. The analysis compared the results of the PROBID method with those of the TOPSIS (Technique for Order Preference by Similarity to Ideal Solution), VIKOR (VIsekriterijumska optimizacija i KOmpromisno Resenje in Serbian), sPROBID, and MOORA (Multi-Objective Optimization based on Ratio Analysis) techniques. TOPSIS is a renowned distance-based MCDM technique which incorporates both the ideal and anti-ideal solution through a utility value to identify the optimum alternative that is nearest to the ideal solution. VIKOR provides a compromise solution, which is closest to the ideal. It ranks the alternatives in ascending order, where the option with the lowest value is considered optimal. The sPROBID technique is a simplified variant of the PROBID approach that employs only the upper and lower quarters of the ideal solutions rather than the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1^{st}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^{nd}$$\end{document} halves to calculate the overall PID and NID. The rankings obtained from the TOPSIS, VIKOR and sPROBID techniques are identical to that of our proposed method. MOORA is a compensatory approach which ranks the alternatives based on their appraisal score, estimated as the difference between the total performance scores of the BF and NBF criteria. The results from the MOORA technique show a considerable difference with our proposed method, in which the first-ranked alternative is placed second and the second-ranked alternative is placed first, while the remaining alternatives retain the same rankings. This might be due to minor methodological variations between the two approaches. To sum up, the comparison study’s findings closely align with our proposed PROBID method, demonstrating its efficiency and consistency. Sensitivity analysis

Sensitivity study is performed to assess the robustness of the decision model by examining how changes in input variables impact its outcome. This work involved three distinct cases of sensitivity analysis to ascertain how variations in the experts’ weighting of the decision matrix affect the ranking of the alternatives.

Case I In this case, the experts’ weighting is determined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {X}$$\end{document} = (1, 0, 0), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_2$$\end{document} ranks first, followed by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_1$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_3$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_4$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_6$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_5$$\end{document}. Here, there is a slight shift in the ranking where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_1$$\end{document} moves to the second position and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_3$$\end{document} to the third, while all other alternatives retain their original positions.

Case II Here the experts’ weighting is established as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {X}$$\end{document} = (0, 1, 0), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_2$$\end{document} holds the first position, followed by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_3$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_1$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_4$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_6$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_5$$\end{document}. This shows that despite changes to the experts’ weights, the ranking remained the same.

Case III The experts’ weighting in this case is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {X}$$\end{document} = (0, 0, 1), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_2$$\end{document} again holds the first position, followed by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_1$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_3$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_4$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_6$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_5$$\end{document}, which is similar to Case I.

In each of the cases discussed above, the alternative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {B}_2$$\end{document}, i.e., rain gardens, takes the first position, indicating the robustness of the proposed methodology. The sensitivity analysis demonstrates that moderate variations in criterion weights do not alter the overall ranking structure, indicating strong model robustness under realistic uncertainty conditions. Environmental aspects, especially stormwater management and heat mitigation, exert the greatest influence on ranking adjustments, underscoring their crucial significance in policy-driven GI selection. From a decision-making standpoint, this suggests that policies prioritizing environmental resilience will constantly favour the same GI practice, even when stakeholder preferences vary. The observed stability of the foremost alternative confirms that the proposed framework provides reliable and feasible guidance for real-world GI planning. Conclusion

This study addresses the problem of selecting an appropriate GI practice for SUD, which is an MCDM problem often involving multiple and conflicting criteria. In this regard, we develop an advanced MCDM model which integrates the D-CRITIC and PROBID approaches. SBFS is employed to represent the experts’ evaluation information in order to eliminate imprecision and uncertainty from the data. The case study’s process featured six kinds of GI practices as alternatives with fourteen evaluation criteria categorized under environmental, economic and social dimensions. The relative significance of each criterion is established utilizing the D-CRITIC weighting approach, and the alternatives are ranked using the PROBID technique, where the rain gardens \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {B}_2)$$\end{document} turned out to be the optimal solution. In addition, the findings of the proposed method were compared to those of other MCDM approaches such as TOPSIS, VIKOR, sPROBID, and MOORA, and a sensitivity analysis was performed by modifying the experts’ weighting of the decision matrix. Overall, this study provides a reliable and efficient approach for communities and stakeholders to evaluate the performance of GI, which may greatly help in developing focused improvement plans for SUD.

Limitations

The proposed work possesses methodological constraints due to its dependence on predefined criteria and fixed aggregation operators, which may limit adaptability to different contexts. Although the expert opinions are systematically aggregated and transformed into fuzzy values, outcomes may be impacted by differences in expert interpretation. Since the current study does not provide a direct real-world application or validation, practical implementation of the suggested work may be constrained. The computational load of the D-CRITIC technique is a potential limitation of this work. Since GI performance and prioritization might vary among locations with various climate, geographic, or governance settings, the reliance on a particular set of GI practices and criteria poses a contextual issue. Furthermore, as the framework reflects a static planning environment and is best suited to local contexts, scaling it to the national level may be challenging.

Implementation solutions

The framework can be strengthened by incorporating the design dimension1 alongside environmental, economic, and social dimensions to better capture practical planning considerations. In addition, applying the model to a real-world case study within a specific study area enhances contextual relevance and supports actionable decision-making45.

Future Work

Future work could involve conducting a real-world, GIS-based case study to support GI selection for SUD. Integrating machine learning technology with the approaches discussed in this study may be a useful strategy for solving crucial decision-making problems. Furthermore, Real-time object detection using deep learning, as demonstrated in the Forklift Automated guided vehicle study47 illustrates how intelligent automation can process dynamic data for contextual decision-making. Integrating such methods could make the proposed framework adaptive, updating priorities in real time to support responsive and sustainable green infrastructure planning48.
